        CB-14
                                        City of St. Petersburg
                          Housing, Land Use, and Transportation Committee
                                   June 11, 2026 Meeting Minutes
                                        City Hall, Room 100

Present: Committee Chair Richie Floyd, Committee Vice-Chair Mike Harting, Council Member Brandi
Gabbard, Council Member Gina Driscoll, and Council Member Corey Givens Jr (Alternate)

Absent: None

Also Present: City Administrator Rob Gerdes, Housing and Neighborhood Services Administrator Amy
Foster, Neighborhood Services Managing Director Joe Waugh, Housing and Community Development
Assistant Director Mark Vanlue, Assistant City Attorney Isabella Sobel, Deputy City Clerk Paul Traci

Staff Liaison: Cortney Phillips – City Council Legislative Aide

1) Call to Order – 8:30 AM
2) Approval of Agenda – VC Harting motioned for approval. All voted in favor.
3) Approval of May 14, 2026 Minutes – CM Driscoll motioned for approval. All voted in favor.
4) New Business – June 11, 2026


Housing Opportunities for All Annual Update – Housing and Neighborhood Services Administrator
Amy Foster

    In connection with the new business, Housing and Neighborhood Services Administrator Amy Foster
provided a PowerPoint presentation on Housing Opportunities for All, including information on market
forces and updates on the strategic plan. Highlights of the presentation included updates on:

    -   Housing production trends based on American Community Survey data from 2010-2024, with
        future moderate and strong growth trends.
    -   Housing statistics, including financial characteristics, homeownership rate, housing units,
        occupancy characteristics, and vacancies
    -   Rental market data, with renter-occupied households at 37% of total households in St. Petersburg
        and owner-occupied households at 63%
    -   Current trends in eviction Filings
    -   Current real estate market summary
    -   Heatmap of LLC owned parcels, for a total 303 properties
    -   Housing Opportunities for All Accomplishments (2020-2025)

    CM Driscoll expressed her concerns with the data regarding average pay for different types of jobs,
and how many of those jobs are at or above the housing wage. She would like to see us close the gap for
someone making minimum wage to be able to afford to rent an apartment. CM Driscoll remarked that it
was disappointing that the needle hasn’t moved toward the percentage of owner-occupied households over
renter-occupied, because for years we've been trying to create more home ownership opportunities for
people who are below 100% AMI. Administrator Foster agreed that if you look at the citywide census
data, it stays relatively flat. However, other local data analysis into zip code areas has reflected that areas
such as Black home ownership has gone up.



                                                                                                             1
    CM Driscoll discussed opportunities to preserve smaller rental properties that the City can purchase
and rehabilitate. Administrator Foster responded that we look at market rate units when we see them and
share them with developer teams to see if they can make the numbers work. In addition to that, we do
have our list of currently affordable properties, and when their affordability periods expire or renovations
need to be made, we have conversations with those owners to try and get those units extended.

    CM Gabbard asked if there was any specific local data, such as Black homeownership, that speaks to
senior ownership and how that may have changed over the course of the last couple of years. She spoke
to her concerns about the senior community in neighborhoods that were affected by the hurricanes.
Administrator Foster replied that she can look into finding relevant publicly available data with the
Housing team. CM Gabbard stressed the importance of aligning our goals with an aging population, as we
can’t build our way out of a landlocked city.

     CM Gabbard discussed that the Riviera Bay Neighborhood Association is making a concerted effort
to prevent LLCs and rental ownership from taking over the neighborhood, with some of the leaders of the
community who have the means purchasing and renovating these properties themselves to sell or rent at
a modest profit. While this is happening on a small scale right now, she believes with support from private
capital partners and whatever the City can do to elevate, there could be a real model there that reflects
what true community resiliency and sustainability looks like.

     CM Harting spoke regarding affordability and emphasized that calculations for total affordability
would need to include things like car payments, bus passes, and other living expenses. He asked if staff
have discussed how the housing department will be impacted should the Florida homestead exemption
expansion pass. City Administrator Gerdes responded that Assistant City Administrator Tom Greene and
his team are running these numbers, and they're looking at the impacts to the general fund, CRA, and TIF.
He went on to note that he believes that the effects to TIF and the South CRA would be very impactful,
but we don't yet have the number on what a possible annual reduction would be.

     CM Givens inquired why the process for the sale of the Deuces Rising has taken so long just to get
us to a sales price that does not include the ongoing construction. Housing and Community Development
Assistant Director Mark Vanlue responded that there are a lot of factors that go into the sale price. The
City is committed to selling those units at an affordable price, half of them to 80% AMI and below, and
half to 120% AMI and below. To make sure that the price we set is truly affordable to those income levels,
we have to consider factors that will go into that sale price, including property tax, insurance, and HOA
costs. We could not structure the HOA and establish the operating and reserve budgets until we're very
close to the end of the project, which we now are, and we're in the process of doing that.

     CM Givens asked what the City’s investment was in the project. Mr. Vanlue responded that the total
amount that's been invested so far is around $700,000-$800,000 per unit. This includes a lot of site work,
some site remediation that had to be done early on due to environmental concerns, and then some of the
prep work that will be credited toward the commercial portion of the project later on. Administrator Foster
added that Council has intentionally passed a number of policies over the years to make sure we hire from
within the CRA and work with small- and minority business owners. All of those policies taken together
increase the cost of doing work, and in the past, this body has felt like those were trade-offs they were
willing to make.



                                                                                                          2
     CM Givens commended staff on their work to lower eviction rates and asked what types of programs
the City is funding for legal aid. Neighborhood Services Managing Director Joe Waugh replied that we
have direct lines of communication to legal aid organizations when we are reaching out to tenants, as the
City is typically the first to notify tenants that they have an eviction. Many times, when we knock on a
tenant’s door, they haven't received a notification yet, so we're getting them a head start on the information
that they need to reach out to those organizations. We do have funding set aside for partnering with a legal
aid organization, and an RFQ was put out a few months ago that did not receive any responses. We ended
up going into direct negotiations with one entity that are still ongoing, and we're hopeful that if we can
come to an agreement, it would then be coming to council in the near future.

     CM Givens questioned what we’re doing to curb slum lords. Mr. Waugh discussed that recently Codes
dedicated to specific staff members just to do interior inspections for tenants, and they can have
conversations with the tenants to understand what challenges they may be facing to connect them with
organizations for assistance. Mr. Waugh spoke about changes on the enforcement side as well, so when
Codes takes cases before the Code Enforcement Board for tenant-occupied properties, we're suggesting a
10-day compliance time and increased fine amounts. The Code Enforcement Board has been supportive
of us bringing those cases. CM Givens asked if there was an update on South CRA probate property
assistance. Mr. Waugh responded that Codes investigators connect residents to probate resources, N-
Team, and housing resources when they see a property is in an estate, so they don't feel like they're in a
position to where the only option is to sell the home.

    Chair Floyd commented that the 2020-2021 data on rental vacancies stood out to him as having higher
than current vacancy rates, which is unexpected and not necessarily aligning with housing trends. He went
on to note that while the area median income has increased by $30,000 in the last five years, the typical
family that lives here has likely not had a $30,000 a year wage increase. While this is indicative of people
moving here from out of the area, it does seem like the trend has slowed down over the last year.

    There being no further business, the meeting was adjourned at 9:41 AM.
